Estimation for change point of discretely observed ergodic diffusion processes
Yozo Tonaki, Yusuke Kaino, Masayuki Uchida

Abstract
. We treat the change point problem in ergodic diffusion processes from discrete observations. Tonaki et al. (2020) proposed adaptive tests for detecting changes in the diffusion and drift parameters in ergodic diffusion models. When any changes are detected by this method, the next question to be considered is where the change point is. Therefore, we propose the method to estimate the change point of the parameter for two cases: the case where there is a change in the diffusion parameter, and the case where there is no change in the diffusion parameter but a change in the drift parameter. Furthermore, we present rates of convergence and distributional results of the change point estimators. Some examples and simulation results are also given.



1.
Introduction

We consider a
d
-dimensional diffusion process
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

satisfying the stochastic differential equation:

(1.1)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\mathrm{d}X_{t}=b(X_{t},\beta)\mathrm{d}t+a(X_{t},\alpha)\mathrm{d}W_{t},\\
X_{0}=x_{0},\end{cases}

where parameter space

\Theta=\Theta_{A}\times\Theta_{B}
, which is a
compact convex subset of

\mathbb{R}^{p}\times\mathbb{R}^{q}
,

\theta=(\alpha,\beta)\in\Theta
is an unknown parameter and

\{W_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

is a
d
-dimensional standard Wiener process.
The diffusion coefficient

a:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{A}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
the drift coefficient
b:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{B}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}

are known except for the parameter
\theta
,
and the true parameter
\theta^{*}=(\alpha^{*},\beta^{*})
belongs to
\mathrm{Int\,}\Theta
.
We assume that the solution of (
1.1
) exists, and

P_{\theta}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\theta}
denote the law of the
solution and the expectation with respect to
P_{\theta}
, respectively.
Let
\{X_{t_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
be discrete observations,
where
t_{i}=t_{i}^{n}=ih_{n}
,
and
\{h_{n}\}
is a positive sequence with
h=h_{n}\longrightarrow 0
,
T=nh\longrightarrow\infty
and

nh^{2}\longrightarrow 0
as
n\to\infty
.

We deal with the change point problem of parameters in diffusion process models.
The change point problem consists of two parts:
detection of the parameter change and estimation of the change point.
For example, suppose that we obtain the paths shown in Figure
2
.
Given such data,
the first thing we would consider
is whether any changes occur in that path.
The paths in Figure
2
clearly shows that some change occurs,
but the paths in Figure
2
may be difficult to find a change.
The first step of the change point problem
is to investigate whether there is a change in the data or not,
which is “detection of the parameter change”.
Suppose that we can detect a change in the paths
in Figure
2
or
2
.
In Figure
2
,
we can visually see that the change occurs at
t=60

in the left figure and at
t=40
in the right figure,
but the paths in Figure
2
are not even visually recognizable,
so we have no idea where the change occurs.
The goal of the change point problem
is to investigate the change point when we see that there is a change in the data,
which is“estimation of the change point”.

Figure 1.

Sample paths of the
hyperbolic diffusion model

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\Bigl(\beta+\frac{\gamma X_{t}}{\sqrt{1+X_{t}^{2}}}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t}

whose parameter changes form

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(0.75,1,3)
to
(1.5,1,3)
at
t=60
(left),
and changes from

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,1,3)
to
(1,-1,3)
at
t=40
(right).

Figure 2.

Sample paths of the
hyperbolic diffusion model

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\Bigl(\beta+\frac{\gamma X_{t}}{\sqrt{1+X_{t}^{2}}}\Bigr)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t}

whose parameter changes form

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,1,3)
to
(1.05,1,3)
at
t=30
(left),
and changes from

(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1,1,3)
to
(1,0.5,3)
at
t=50
(right).

The change point problem for diffusion processes from discrete observations
has been studied by many researchers.
For non-ergodic diffusion processes, see
De Gregorio and Iacus (2008)
and
Iacus and Yoshida (2012).
For ergodic diffusion processes, we can refer to
Song and Lee (2009),
Lee (2011),
Negri and Nishiyama (2017),
Song (2020),
and
Tonaki et al. (2020).
De Gregorio and Iacus (2008)
considered the detection of the parameter change in diffusion parameter
and the estimation of the change point,
and Iacus and Yoshida (2012)
studied the estimating problem of the change point
in diffusion parameter.
In contrast,
Song and Lee (2009),
Lee (2011)
and Song (2020)
considered the detection of the parameter change in diffusion parameter,
and
Negri and Nishiyama (2017)
and Tonaki et al. (2020)
considered the detection of the parameter change in diffusion and drift parameters.
Specifically,
Negri and Nishiyama (2017)
proposed simultaneous test for changes in diffusion and drift parameters,
and
Tonaki et al. (2020)
proposed adaptive tests for those changes.

When we consider the change point problem of the diffusion processes,
we first need to investigate whether there are any change points in the given data.
Negri and Nishiyama (2017) considered the following hypothesis testing problem:

H_{0}^{\theta}:\theta=(\alpha,\beta)
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
v.s.
H_{1}^{\theta}:
not
H_{0}^{\theta}
.

They considered the simultaneous test for changes in diffusion and drift parameters,
but even if any changes are detected,
we can not determine which parameter changed.
In order to solve this problem,
Tonaki et al. (2020)
considered the adaptive tests for changes in diffusion and drift parameters.
Specifically, consider the following steps.
First, consider the following hypothesis testing problem.

H_{0}^{\alpha}:\alpha
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
v.s.
H_{1}^{\alpha}:
not
H_{0}^{\alpha}
.

If
H_{0}^{\alpha}
is not rejected,
then consider the following hypothesis testing problem.

H_{0}^{\beta}:\beta
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
v.s.
H_{1}^{\beta}:
not
H_{0}^{\beta}
.

When we find that there is a change,
the next task is to estimate the change point.
Since the study of the estimation of the change point
for ergodic diffusion processes is still in its infancy,
we consider the estimation of the change point for ergodic diffusion processes.
With the adaptive detection method,
when
H_{0}^{\alpha}
is rejected,
we can consider the estimation of the change point of the diffusion parameter
\alpha
,
and when
H_{0}^{\alpha}
is not rejected
and
H_{0}^{\beta}
is rejected,
we can consider the estimation of the change point of the drift parameter
\beta
,
which brings us one step closer to our goal.

This paper is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we state the main results.
We propose change point estimators for diffusion and drift parameters
and present rates of convergence and distributional results
of change point estimators.
Section
3

discusses the nuisance parameters of the proposed estimators.
Section
4

presents the powers of tests proposed by
Tonaki et al. (2020).
In Sections
5
and
6
,
we give some examples and simulation studies.
Finally, we provide the proofs in Section
7
.

2.
Main Results

We consider the following two situations:

Situation I.

\alpha^{*}
changes in
0\leq t\leq T
, that is,
there exists
\tau_{*}^{\alpha}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{*},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T),\\
\alpha_{2}^{*},\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T],\end{cases}

where
\alpha_{1}^{*},\alpha_{2}^{*}\in\mathrm{Int}\Theta_{A}
,
\alpha_{1}^{*}\neq\alpha_{2}^{*}
.
Now (
1.1
) can be expressed as follows.

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}a(X_{s},\alpha_{1}^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T)\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}a(X_{s},\alpha_{2}^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s}.\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T]\end{cases}

Situation II.

\alpha^{*}
does not change
and

\beta^{*}
changes
in
0\leq t\leq T
, that is,
there exists
\tau_{*}^{\beta}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\beta^{*}=\begin{cases}\beta_{1}^{*},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\beta_{2}^{*},\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\end{cases}

where
\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*}\in\mathrm{Int}\Theta_{B}
,
\beta_{1}^{*}\neq\beta_{2}^{*}
.
Now (
1.1
) can be expressed as follows.

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta_{1}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}a(X_{s},\alpha^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T)\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta_{2}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}a(X_{s},\alpha^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s}.\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T]\end{cases}

We consider the following two cases:

Case A.

The parameters
\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}

(resp.
\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
)
depend on
n
in Situation I (resp. II),

Case B.

The parameters
\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}

(resp.
\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
)
are fixed and not depend on
n
in Situation I (resp. II).

For matrices
c\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d_{2}}
,
we write
c^{\otimes 2}=cc^{\mathsf{T}}
,
where
c^{\mathsf{T}}
is the transpose of
c
.
Let
A(x,\alpha)=a(x,\alpha)^{\otimes 2}
and
\Delta X_{i}=X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}
.
Let
C^{k,\ell}_{\uparrow}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta)
be
the space of all functions
f
satisfying the following conditions:

(i)

f
is continuously differentiable with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
up to
order
k
for all
\theta\in\Theta
;

(ii)

f
and all its
x
-derivatives up to order
k
are

\ell
times continuously differentiable with respect to
\theta\in\Theta
;

(iii)

f
and all derivatives are of polynomial growth in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

uniformly in
\theta\in\Theta
, i.e.,

g
is of polynomial growth in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
uniformly
in
\theta\in\Theta
if, for some
C>0
, we have

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|g(x,\theta)\|\leq C(1+\|x\|)^{C}.

We assume the following conditions:

[C1]

There exists a constant
C
such that for any
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\alpha\in\Theta_{A}}\|a(x,\alpha)-a(y,\alpha)\|+\sup_{\beta\in\Theta_{B}}\|b(x,\beta)-b(y,\beta)\|\leq C\|x-y\|.

[C2]

\displaystyle\sup_{t}\mathbb{E}_{\theta}\left[\|X_{t}\|^{k}\right]<\infty

for all
k\geq 0
and
\theta\in\Theta
.

[C3]

\displaystyle\inf_{x,\alpha}\det\left(A(x,\alpha)\right)>0
.

[C4]

a\in C^{4,4}_{\uparrow}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{A})

and

b\in C^{4,4}_{\uparrow}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{B})
.

[C5]

The solution of (
1.1
) is ergodic with its invariant measure
\mu_{\theta}

such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|x\|^{k}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta}(x)<\infty\quad\text{for all }\ k\geq 0\text{ and }\theta\in\Theta,

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta_{n}}(x)\longrightarrow\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta_{0}}(x)\quad\text{for all measurable function }f

as
\theta_{n}\longrightarrow\theta_{0}
.

2.1.
Estimation for the diffusion parameter

First, we consider Situation I, that is, the estimation for the diffusion parameter.
Write
\vartheta_{\alpha}=|\alpha_{1}^{*}-\alpha_{2}^{*}|
and let

\displaystyle\Xi^{\alpha}(x,\alpha)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\mathrm{tr}\bigl(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha^{\ell_{1}}}AA^{-1}\partial_{\alpha^{\ell_{2}}}A(x,\alpha)\bigr)\Bigr]_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{p},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\bigl(A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{1})A(x,\alpha_{2})-I_{d}\bigr)-\log\det A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{1})A(x,\alpha_{2}).

Now we additionally assume the following conditions:

[C6-I]

There exist estimators
\hat{\alpha}_{k}=\hat{\alpha}_{k,n}\ (k=1,2)
such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*})=O_{p}(1).

[A1-I]

\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
,
and

\vartheta_{\alpha}=\vartheta_{\alpha,n}
satisfies the following.

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0,\quad n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty,\quad\frac{h}{\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}\longrightarrow\infty,\quad T\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
, and

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{k}^{*}-\alpha_{0})=O(1)
, where
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{A}
,

[A2-I]

For the following three functions
and for any
r\in(1,2)
such that
nh^{r}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\max_{[n^{1/r}]\leq k\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+k}f(X_{t_{i-1}})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(x)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

(a)

\Xi^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{0})
,

(b)

\partial_{\alpha}\Xi^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{0})
,

(c)

\partial_{\alpha}^{3}\Bigl(\mathrm{tr}(A^{-1}(x,\alpha)A(x,\alpha_{0}))-\log\det A^{-1}(x,\alpha)A(x,\alpha_{0})\Bigr)\Bigr|_{\alpha=\alpha_{0}}
.

[B1-I]

\displaystyle\inf_{x}\Gamma^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{1}^{*},\alpha_{2}^{*})>0
.

[B2-I]

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

(a)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha_{k}}\bigl|\partial_{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\Gamma^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})\bigr|<C
,

(b)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha_{k}}\left|\left[\mathrm{tr}\Bigl(\bigl(A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{1})-A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{2})\bigr)\partial_{\alpha^{\ell}}A(x,\alpha_{3})\Bigr)\right]_{\ell=1}^{p}\right|<C
,

(c)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\theta}|Q(x,\theta)|<C
,

where

Q(x,\theta)
is the coefficient of
h^{2}
of
\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}})^{\otimes 2}|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}]
,
that is,

\mathbb{E}_{\theta}[(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}})^{\otimes 2}|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}]=hA(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)+h^{2}Q(X_{t_{i-1}},\theta)+\cdots
.
Here,
\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}=\sigma\left[\{W_{s}\}_{s\leq t_{i}^{n}}\right]
.

See Section
3
for how to construct the estimators
\hat{\alpha}_{k}

that satisfy
[C6-I]
.
If
a(x,\alpha)=\sigma(x)c(\alpha)

for

\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

c:\mathbb{R}^{p}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
then
[A2-I]
,
[B1-I]
and
[B2-I]
(a),(b)
hold because the functions of (a)-(c) of
[A2-I]
,

\Gamma^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})
,

\partial_{(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})}\Gamma^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})

and

\displaystyle\left[\mathrm{tr}\Bigl(\bigl(A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{1})-A^{-1}(x,\alpha_{2})\bigr)\partial_{\alpha^{\ell}}A(x,\alpha_{3})\Bigr)\right]_{\ell=1}^{p}

do not depend on
x
.
Therefore, the Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and hyperbolic diffusion model,
which will be described later in Section
5
,
are models that satisfy
[A2-I]
,
and these models are examples in Case A.
Hyperbolic diffusion model also satisfies
[B2-I]
(c),
and this model is an example in Case B, see Section
5.5
.
If the diffusion coefficient is
a(x,\alpha)=\alpha
, then

[A1-I]
and
[A2-I]
can be reduced to the following condition:

[A1’-I]

\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
,
and

\vartheta_{\alpha}=\vartheta_{\alpha,n}
satisfies the following.

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0,\quad n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty,\quad T\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
, and

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{k}^{*}-\alpha_{0})=O(1)
, where
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{A}
.

Therefore, if
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
after the parameter change
is stationary, then

[A1-I]
and
[A2-I]
can be reduced to the following condition:

[A1”-I]

\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
,
and

\vartheta_{\alpha}=\vartheta_{\alpha,n}
satisfies the following.

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0,\quad n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty,\quad\frac{h}{\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}\longrightarrow\infty

as
n\to\infty
, and

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{k}^{*}-\alpha_{0})=O(1)
, where
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{A}
,

That is, if
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
after the parameter change
is stationary and we assume
[A1’-I]
,
then
[A1-I]
and
[A2-I]
hold, see Section
5.1
.

In Situation I, we set

\displaystyle F_{i}(\alpha)=\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha),

\displaystyle\Phi_{n}(\tau:\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\sum_{i=1}^{[n\tau]}F_{i}(\alpha_{1})+\sum_{i=[n\tau]+1}^{n}F_{i}(\alpha_{2})

and
propose

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}=\underset{\tau\in[0,1]}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\Phi_{n}(\tau:\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2})

as an estimator of
\tau_{*}^{\alpha}
.

In Case A, we set for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{1}^{*}-\alpha_{2}^{*}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e_{\alpha}^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Xi^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(x)e_{\alpha},

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}(v)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}(v)+\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}|v|,

where
\mathcal{W}
is a two sided standard Wiener process.

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
and
[C6-I]
hold in Situation I.
Then, under
[A1-I]
and
[A2-I]
in Case A,

\displaystyle n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{v\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\mathbb{F}(v).

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
and
[C6-I]

hold in Situation I.
Then,
under
[B1-I]
and
[B2-I]
in Case B,

\displaystyle n(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})=O_{p}(1).

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
and
[C6-I]

hold in Situation I.
If for
\epsilon_{1}\in[0,1)
there exists
\delta_{1}\in(0,\frac{1-\epsilon_{1}}{2})

such that
nh^{1/(\epsilon_{1}+\delta_{1})}\longrightarrow 0
, then,
under
[B1-I]
,
[B2-I]
(a) and (b) in Case B,

\displaystyle n^{\epsilon_{1}}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})=o_{p}(1).

Particularly, for
\epsilon_{1}\in[0,\frac{1}{2})
there exists always
\delta_{1}

regardless of
h
.

Although we could not specify the distribution of
n(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})

in Case B, we have strong results in this case.
Actually, in Case B,
we see
\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha}=O_{p}(n^{-1})
.
However, if we choose
\vartheta_{\alpha}=n^{-\nu}
,
0<\nu<\frac{1}{2}
that satisfies
[A1-I]
,
we have

n^{1-2\nu}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})=O_{p}(1)

in Case A, but

n^{1-2\nu}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})=o_{p}(1)

in Case B.

2.2.
Estimation for the drift parameter

Next, we consider Situation II, that is, the estimation for the drift parameter.
Write
\vartheta_{\beta}=|\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*}|
and let

\displaystyle\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\Bigl[\partial_{\beta^{\ell_{1}}}b(x,\beta)^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(x,\alpha)\partial_{\beta^{\ell_{2}}}b(x,\beta)\Bigr]_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}=1}^{q},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\bigl[A^{-1}(x,\alpha)(b(x,\beta_{1})-b(x,\beta_{2}))^{\otimes 2}\bigr].

Now we additionally assume the following conditions:

[C6-II]

There exist estimators

\hat{\alpha}=\hat{\alpha}_{n}
,
\hat{\beta}_{k}=\hat{\beta}_{k,n}\ (k=1,2)

such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}-\alpha^{*})=O_{p}(1),\quad\sqrt{T}(\hat{\beta}_{k}-\beta_{k}^{*})=O_{p}(1).

[A1-II]

\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
and

\vartheta_{\beta}=\vartheta_{\beta,n}
satisfies the following.

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\beta}\longrightarrow 0,\quad T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty,\quad T\vartheta_{\beta}^{4}\longrightarrow 0

as
n\to\infty
, and

\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{k}^{*}-\beta_{0})=O(1)
, where
\beta_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{B}
.

[A2-II]

For the following three functions
and for any
r\in(1,2)
such that
nh^{r}\longrightarrow\infty
,

\displaystyle\max_{[n^{1/r}]\leq k\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\beta}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\beta}]+k}f(X_{t_{i-1}})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})}(x)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

(a)

\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})
,

(b)

\partial_{\beta}\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})
,

(c)

\partial_{\beta}^{3}\Bigl(\mathrm{tr}(A^{-1}(x,\alpha^{*})(b(x,\beta)-b(x,\beta_{0}))^{\otimes 2}\Bigr)\Bigr|_{\beta=\beta_{0}}
.

[A3-II]

There exists an integer
m\geq 4
such that

n^{m/2-1}h^{(m-1)/2}\longrightarrow\infty
,
h^{-1/2}\vartheta_{\beta}^{m-2}\longrightarrow 0

and

b\in C_{\uparrow}^{4,m}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{B})
.

[B1-II]

\displaystyle\inf_{x}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\beta_{1}^{*},\beta_{2}^{*})>0
.

[B2-II]

There exists a constant
C>0
such that

(a)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha,\beta_{k}}\left|\partial_{(\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\beta_{1},\beta_{2})\right|<C
,

(b)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha,\beta_{k}}\left|\Bigl[\mathrm{tr}\Bigl(A^{-1}(x,\alpha)\partial_{\beta^{\ell}}b(x,\beta_{1})\bigl(b(x,\beta_{2})-b(x,\beta_{3})\bigr)^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigr)\Bigr]_{\ell=1}^{q}\right|<C
.

See Section
3
for how to construct the estimators
\hat{\beta}_{k}

that satisfy
[C6-II]
.
If
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
after the parameter change is stationary, then

[A1-II]
and
[A2-II]
can be reduced to the following condition:

[A1’-II]

\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
and

\vartheta_{\beta}=\vartheta_{\beta,n}
satisfies the following.

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\beta}\longrightarrow 0,\quad T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty

as
n\to\infty
, and

\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{k}^{*}-\beta_{0})=O(1)
, where
\beta_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{B}
.

That is, if
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
after the parameter change
is stationary and we assume
[A1’-II]
,
then
[A1-II]
and
[A2-II]
hold, see Section
5.1
.
The hyperbolic diffusion model is one of the models that satisfy

[B1-II]
and
[B2-II]
, see Section
5.5
.

In Situation II, we set

\displaystyle G_{i}(\beta|\alpha)=\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i}-hb(X_{t_{i-1}},\beta))^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right),

\displaystyle\Psi_{n}(\tau:\beta_{1},\beta_{2}|\alpha)=\sum_{i=1}^{[n\tau]}G_{i}(\beta_{1}|\alpha)+\sum_{i=[n\tau]+1}^{n}G_{i}(\beta_{2}|\alpha)

and propose

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}=\underset{\tau\in[0,1]}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\Psi_{n}(\tau:\hat{\beta}_{1},\hat{\beta}_{2}|\hat{\alpha})

as an estimator of
\tau_{*}^{\beta}
.

In Case A, we set for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle e_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*}),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=e_{\beta}^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})}(x)e_{\beta},

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(v)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{J}_{\beta}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}(v)+\mathcal{J}_{\beta}|v|.

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
and
[C6-II]
hold in Situation II.
Then, under
[A1-II]
-
[A3-II]
in Case A,

\displaystyle T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{v\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\mathbb{G}(v).

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
and
[C6-II]
hold in Situation II.
Then,
under
[B1-II]
,
[B2-II]
in Case B,

\displaystyle T(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})=O_{p}(1).

Although we could not specify the distribution of
T(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})

in Case B, we have strong results in this case.
Actually, in Case B,
we see
\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta}=O_{p}(T^{-1})
.
However, if we choose
\vartheta_{\beta}=T^{-\nu}
,
0<\nu<\frac{1}{2}
that satisfies
[A1-II]
,
we have

T^{1-2\nu}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})=O_{p}(1)

in Case A, but

T^{1-2\nu}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})=o_{p}(1)

in Case B.

3.
Estimation of the nuisance parameters
\alpha_{k}^{*}
,
\beta_{k}^{*}

When the values of the parameters
\alpha_{k}^{*}
,
\beta_{k}^{*}
are unknown,
it is necessary to estimate the parameters
\alpha_{k}^{*}
,
\beta_{k}^{*}

for the change point estimation.
In this section, we will discuss estimation of nuisance parameters

\alpha_{k}^{*}
,
\beta_{k}^{*}
.

First, we need the following information to estimate
\alpha_{k}^{*}
or
\beta_{k}^{*}
:

[D1]

There exist
\underline{\tau}^{\alpha},\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}\in(0,1)
such that

\tau_{*}^{\alpha}\in[\underline{\tau}^{\alpha},\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}]
.

[D2]

There exist
\underline{\tau}^{\beta},\overline{\tau}^{\beta}\in(0,1)
such that

\tau_{*}^{\beta}\in[\underline{\tau}^{\beta},\overline{\tau}^{\beta}]
.

If this information is obtained,
since there are no change points in the intervals

[0,\underline{\tau}^{\alpha}T]
and
[\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}T,T]
,
we can estimate
\alpha_{1}^{*}
from the data of
[0,\underline{\tau}^{\alpha}T]

and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
from the data of
[\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}T,T]
.
For example,
we can construct estimators that satisfy
[C6-I]
or
[C6-II]

by the following procedures.
In Situation I, set

\displaystyle\underline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{[n\underline{\tau}^{\alpha}]}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\Biggr),

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}]+1}^{n}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A(X_{t_{i-1}},\alpha)\Biggr),

and let

\hat{\alpha}_{1}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\alpha}\,\underline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)
,
and

\hat{\alpha}_{2}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\alpha}\,\overline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)

as estimators of
\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
, respectively.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*})=O_{p}(1),\quad\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*})=O_{p}(1).

Similarly in Situation II,
set

\displaystyle U_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A_{i-1}(\alpha)\Biggr),

\displaystyle\underline{U}_{n}^{(2)}(\beta|\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{[n\underline{\tau}^{\beta}]}\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i}-hb_{i-1}(\beta))^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right),

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{n}^{(2)}(\beta|\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\overline{\tau}^{\beta}]+1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\alpha)\frac{(\Delta X_{i}-hb_{i-1}(\beta))^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right),

and let

\hat{\alpha}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\alpha}\,U_{n}^{(1)}(\alpha)
,

\hat{\beta}_{1}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\beta}\,\underline{U}_{n}^{(2)}(\beta|\hat{\alpha})
,
and

\hat{\beta}_{2}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\beta}\,\overline{U}_{n}^{(2)}(\beta|\hat{\alpha})

as estimators of
\alpha^{*}
,
\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
, respectively.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\sqrt{T}(\hat{\beta}_{1}-\beta_{1}^{*})=O_{p}(1),\quad\sqrt{T}(\hat{\beta}_{2}-\beta_{2}^{*})=O_{p}(1).

Thus, if
[D1]
or
[D2]
is satisfied,
then we can construct estimators that satisfy
[C6-I]
or
[C6-II]
.
Next, let us discuss how to find
\underline{\tau}
and
\overline{\tau}

that satisfy
[D1]
or
[D2]
.
To find these,
we need some method to detect changes in the diffusion or drift parameters.
However, in Situations I and II,
the adaptive tests for changes in diffusion and drift parameters proposed
by Tonaki et al. (2020)
makes it possible.
Namely, we can detect a change in the diffusion or drift parameters
in the interval
[\tau_{1}T,\tau_{2}T]

by the following test statistics
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
,

\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
and
\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2d([n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}])}}\max_{1\leq k\leq[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\left|\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{1}]+k}\hat{\eta}_{i}-\frac{k}{[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{2}]}\hat{\eta}_{i}\right|,

\displaystyle\hat{\eta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{dT(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}}\max_{1\leq k\leq[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\left|\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{1}]+k}\hat{\xi}_{i}-\frac{k}{[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{2}]}\hat{\xi}_{i}\right|,

\displaystyle\hat{\xi}_{i}

\displaystyle=1_{d}^{\mathsf{T}}a^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}-hb(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})}}\max_{1\leq k\leq[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{n}^{-1/2}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\left(\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{1}]+k}\hat{\zeta}_{i}-\frac{k}{[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{2}]}\hat{\zeta}_{i}\right)\right\|,

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}-hb(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}(\tau_{1},\tau_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{[n\tau_{2}]-[n\tau_{1}]}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{1}]+1}^{[n\tau_{2}]}\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta}).

Below we describe how to find
\underline{\tau}
and
\overline{\tau}
.

First, the assumption is that a change is detected in the interval
[0,T]
.

U-1)

Choose
\tau_{1}^{U}\in(0,1)
(e.g.,
\tau_{1}^{U}=3/4
), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[0,\tau_{1}^{U}T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\overline{\tau}=\tau_{1}^{U}
and go to L-1).

(b)

If not detected, go to U-2).

U-k)

Choose
\tau_{k}^{U}\in(\tau_{k-1}^{U},1)
(e.g.,
\tau_{k}^{U}=1-2^{-(k+1)}
), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[0,\tau_{k}^{U}T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\overline{\tau}=\tau_{k}^{U}
and go to L-1).

(b)

If not detected, go to U-
(k+1)
).

Assume that we chose
\tau_{k}^{U}
as
\overline{\tau}
in U-k).

L-1)

Choose
\tau_{1}^{L}\in(0,\tau_{k}^{U})
(e.g.,
\tau_{1}^{L}=1/4
or
\tau_{k-1}^{U}
), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[\tau_{1}^{L}T,T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\underline{\tau}=\tau_{1}^{L}
.

(b)

If not detected, go to L-2).

L-m)

Choose
\tau_{m}^{L}\in(0,\tau_{m-1}^{L})

(e.g.,
\tau_{m}^{L}=2^{-(m+1)}
or
\tau_{k-m}^{L}
(if
k>m
)), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[\tau_{m}^{L}T,T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\underline{\tau}=\tau_{m}^{L}
.

(b)

If not detected, go to L-
(m+1)
).

We may also choose
\underline{\tau}
and
\overline{\tau}
at the same time,
that is,

1)

Choose
\tau_{1}\in(0,1/2)
(e.g.,
\tau_{1}=1/4
), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[\tau_{1}T,(1-\tau_{1})T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\underline{\tau}=\tau_{1}
and
\overline{\tau}=1-\tau_{1}
.

(b)

If not detected, go to 2).

k)

Choose
\tau_{k}\in(0,\tau_{k-1})

(e.g.,
\tau_{k}=2^{-(k+1)}
), and
investigate the change point in the interval
[\tau_{k}T,(1-\tau_{k})T]
.

(a)

If a change is detected,
set
\underline{\tau}=\tau_{k}
and
\overline{\tau}=1-\tau_{k}
.

(b)

If not detected, go to
k+1
).

We can choose
\underline{\tau}
and
\overline{\tau}
in the above manner.

4.
The powers of tests in Case A

As we saw in Section
3
,
we need estimators of the nuisance parameters
\alpha_{k}^{*}
and
\beta_{k}^{*}

when estimating the change point,
and have to find
\underline{\tau}
and
\overline{\tau}

that satisfy
[D1]
or
[D2]
in order to construct the estimators.
In Section
3
,
we tried to find them using the adaptive tests proposed by
Tonaki et al. (2020).
When using those tests,
it is necessary to check whether the consistency holds.
However, the consistency of the tests in Case B was mentioned,
but not in Case A.
Thus, in this section,
we discuss the consistency of the following tests

\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
,
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}
and
\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}

in Case A.

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2dn}}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\eta}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\eta}_{i}\right|,\quad\hat{\eta}_{i}=\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{dT}}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\xi}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\xi}_{i}\right|,\quad\hat{\xi}_{i}=1_{d}^{\mathsf{T}}a^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}-hb(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\max_{1\leq k\leq n}\left\|\mathcal{I}_{n}^{-1/2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\hat{\zeta}_{i}-\frac{k}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\zeta}_{i}\right)\right\|,

\displaystyle\hat{\zeta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})(X_{t_{i}}-X_{t_{i-1}}-hb(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})),

\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{n}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\alpha})\partial_{\beta}b(X_{t_{i-1}},\hat{\beta}).

First, we consider the power of the test for the diffusion parameter
\alpha
,
that is, the following hypothesis testing problem:

H_{0}^{\alpha}
:
\alpha^{*}
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
,

H_{1}^{\alpha}
: There exists
\tau_{*}^{\alpha}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\alpha^{*}=\begin{cases}\alpha_{1}^{*},&t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T),\\
\alpha_{2}^{*},&t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T],\end{cases}

where

\alpha_{1}^{*}
and
\alpha_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
,
and

\alpha_{1}^{*}\neq\alpha_{2}^{*}
. Let
\vartheta_{\alpha}=|\alpha_{1}^{*}-\alpha_{2}^{*}|
.
Now, we assume the following conditions:

[E1]

Under
H_{1}^{\alpha}
,

\vartheta_{\alpha}\longrightarrow 0
and
n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
,
and
there exist
\alpha_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{A}
and
c_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}

such that

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{k}^{*}-\alpha_{0})\longrightarrow c_{k}

for
k=1,2
.

[E2]

Under
H_{1}^{\alpha}
,
there exists an estimator
\hat{\alpha}
of
\alpha_{0}
such that

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\hat{\alpha}-\alpha_{0})=O_{p}(1)
.

[E3]

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[\mathrm{tr}\bigl(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha^{\ell}}A(x,\alpha_{0})\bigr)\right]_{\ell}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(x)(c_{1}-c_{2})\neq 0

under
H_{1}^{\alpha}
.

1-dimensional Ornstein-Ulenbeck process and hyperbolic diffusion model are examples of models
which satisfy
[E2]
and
[E3]
.
See Section
5.2
for some discussion of estimator
\hat{\alpha}

that satisfies
[E2]
.
See Section
5.3
for models that satisfy
[E3]
.

Let
\epsilon\in(0,1)
and
w_{k}(\epsilon)
denote the upper-
\epsilon
point of

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}\|\boldsymbol{B}_{k}^{0}(s)\|
,
that is,

\displaystyle P(\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}\|\boldsymbol{B}_{k}^{0}(s)\|>w_{k}(\epsilon))=\epsilon
.

Assume
[C1]
-
[C5]
,

[E1]
-
[E3]
.
Then, under
H_{1}^{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle P\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}>w_{1}(\epsilon)\bigr)\longrightarrow 1,

that is, the test
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
is consistent.

In the following, we assume that
\alpha^{*}
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
.
We consider the power of the test for the drift parameter
\beta
,
that is,
the following hypothesis testing problem:

H_{0}^{\beta}
:
\beta^{*}
does not change over
0\leq t\leq T
,

H_{1}^{\beta}
: There exists
\tau_{*}^{\beta}\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle\beta^{*}=\begin{cases}\beta_{1}^{*},&t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\beta_{2}^{*},&t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\end{cases}

where

\beta_{1}^{*}
and
\beta_{2}^{*}
depend on
n
,
and
\beta_{1}^{*}\neq\beta_{2}^{*}
. Let
\vartheta_{\beta}=|\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*}|
.
Now, we assume the following conditions:

[F1]

Under
H_{1}^{\beta}
,

\vartheta_{\beta}\longrightarrow 0
and
T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}\longrightarrow\infty
as
n\to\infty
,
and
there exist
\beta_{0}\in\mathrm{Int}\,\Theta_{B}
and
d_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{q}

such that

\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{k}^{*}-\beta_{0})\longrightarrow d_{k}

for
k=1,2
.

[F2]

Under
H_{1}^{\beta}
,
there exist

\bar{\beta}^{*}
with
\bar{\beta}^{*}-\beta_{0}=O(\vartheta_{\beta})

and
estimators
\hat{\alpha}
,
\hat{\beta}
such that

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}-\alpha^{*})=O_{p}(1),\quad\sqrt{T}(\hat{\beta}-\bar{\beta}^{*})=O_{p}(1).

[F3]

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}1_{d}^{\mathsf{T}}a^{-1}(x,\alpha^{*})\partial_{\beta}b(x,\beta_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\alpha^{*},\beta_{0})}(x)(d_{1}-d_{2})\neq 0

under
H_{1}^{\beta}
.

See
Kessler (1995,1997),
Uchida and Yoshida (2011, 2012),
Tonaki et al. (2020)
for how to construct the estimators
\hat{\alpha}
and
\hat{\beta}
that satisfy

[F2]
.
See Section
5.4
and
5.5
for models that satisfy
[F3]
.

Assume
[C1]
-
[C5]
,

[F1]
-
[F3]
.
Then, under
H_{1}^{\beta}
,

\displaystyle P\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}>w_{1}(\epsilon)\bigr)\longrightarrow 1.

We additionally assume the following condition:

[F4]

There exists an integer
M\geq 3
such that
h^{-1/2}\vartheta_{\beta}^{M-1}\longrightarrow 0

and
b\in C_{\uparrow}^{4,M}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta_{B})
.

Assume
[C1]
-
[C5]
,

[F1]
,
[F2]
and
[F4]
.
Then, under
H_{1}^{\beta}
,

\displaystyle P\bigl(\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}>w_{q}(\epsilon)\bigr)\longrightarrow 1.

For the consistency of the test
\mathcal{T}_{2,n}^{\beta}
,
only
[F1]
,
[F2]
and
[F4]
are sufficient.
This is because

[F3’]

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\beta}b(x,\beta_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(x,\alpha^{*})\partial_{\beta}b(x,\beta_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta_{0}}(x)(d_{1}-d_{2})\neq 0
,

which corresponds to
[F3]
,
is always valid since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\partial_{\beta}b(x,\beta_{0})^{\mathsf{T}}A^{-1}(x,\alpha^{*})\partial_{\beta}b(x,\beta_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\beta_{0}}(x)

is regular and

\displaystyle|d_{1}-d_{2}|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{0})-\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{2}^{*}-\beta_{0})|=\lim_{n\to\infty}|\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*})|=1\neq 0.

5.
Examples

5.1.
Sufficient condition of assumptions [A2-I] and [A2-II]

A process
\{X_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
with a single change point can be expressed as follows.
There exists a process
\{\tilde{X}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
such that

X_{t}^{1}=\tilde{X}_{t}(\theta_{1}^{*})
,

X_{0}^{1}=x_{0}^{1}
,

X_{t}^{2}=\tilde{X}_{t}(\theta_{2}^{*})
,

X_{0}^{2}=x_{0}^{2}
,

X_{\tau T}^{1}=X_{\tau T}^{2}

and

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}X_{t}^{1},\quad 0\leq t<\tau T,\\
X_{t}^{2},\quad\tau T\leq t\leq T.\end{cases}

If
\{X_{t}^{2}\}_{t\geq 0}
is stationary and
\theta_{2}^{*}\longrightarrow\theta_{0}
, then
we have, for
f\in C_{\uparrow}^{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
,

\displaystyle\max_{[n^{1/r}]\leq k\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=[n\tau]+1}^{[n\tau]+k}f(X_{t_{i-1}})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta_{0}}(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\max_{[n^{1/r}]\leq k\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=[n\tau]+1}^{[n\tau]+k}f(X_{t_{i-1}}^{2})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta_{0}}(x)\right|

\displaystyle{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{=}}}\max_{[n^{1/r}]\leq k\leq n}\left|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}f(X_{t_{i-1}}^{2})-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\theta_{0}}(x)\right|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

and
[A2-I]
or
[A2-II]
holds.

5.2.
Model that satisfies the assumption [E2]

As an example of the model that satisfies
[E2]
,
we consider the
d
-dimensional diffusion process

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(X_{s})\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T)\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}b(X_{s},\beta)\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}\sigma(X_{s})\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}^{*})\mathrm{d}W_{s},\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T]\end{cases}

where

\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\delta(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})
,

\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}>0
.
The true value of the parameters are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{1,1}^{*}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{1,d}^{*}\end{array}\right),\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{2,1}^{*}\\
\vdots\\
\alpha_{2,d}^{*}\end{array}\right),

which convergence to
\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}=(\alpha_{0,1},\ldots,\alpha_{0,d})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
We define

\displaystyle U^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Biggr)

and set the estimator

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,U^{(1)}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
Then, we have

(5.1)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0})=O_{p}(1).

Proof of (
5.1
).

We see

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{j}}U_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\frac{2}{\alpha_{j}}\left(\frac{-1}{\alpha_{j}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}\left([\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}\delta(\mathrm{e}_{j})\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{-1}\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+n\right),

where
\mathrm{e}_{j}=(0,\ldots,1,\ldots,0)^{\mathsf{T}}
.
Then, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}\left([\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}\delta(\mathrm{e}_{j})\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{-1}\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)}

On the other hand, we define

\displaystyle\underline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Biggr),

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{n}\Biggl(\mathrm{tr}\left(A_{i-1}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)+\log\det A_{i-1}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})\Biggr)

and set

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{1}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\underline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
,

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{2}=\mathrm{arginf}_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\,\overline{U}_{n}^{(1)}(\boldsymbol{\alpha})
.
In the same way, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}\sum_{i=1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}\mathrm{tr}\left([\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}\delta(\mathrm{e}_{j})\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{-1}\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)},

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{n-[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{n}\mathrm{tr}\left([\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}\delta(\mathrm{e}_{j})\sigma(X_{t_{i-1}})^{-1}\frac{(\Delta X_{i})^{\otimes 2}}{h}\right)}.

Noting that

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0})=O_{p}(1)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{j}=\sqrt{\frac{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}{n}\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}^{2}+\frac{n-[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}{n}\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}^{2}},

we obtain

\displaystyle\|\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}\|\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}|\hat{\alpha}_{j}-\alpha_{0,j}|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}^{2}-\alpha_{0,j}^{2}|}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}\left|\frac{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}^{2}-\alpha_{0,j}^{2})+\frac{n-[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]}{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}^{2}-\alpha_{0,j}^{2})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{1}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}\left(|\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}-\alpha_{0,j}||\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}+\alpha_{0,j}|+|\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}-\alpha_{0,j}||\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}+\alpha_{0,j}|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\vartheta_{\alpha}\left(\frac{|\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}|\hat{\alpha}_{1,j}-\alpha_{0,j}|+\frac{|\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}|\hat{\alpha}_{2,j}-\alpha_{0,j}|\right).

From
\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
and

\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}
,
we have

\dfrac{|\hat{\alpha}_{k,j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}{|\hat{\alpha}_{j}+\alpha_{0,j}|}=O_{p}(1)

and

\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\hat{\boldsymbol{\alpha}}-\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0})=O_{p}(1)
.
∎

From the above,
1-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and hyperbolic diffusion model
are models which satisfy
[E2]

because the diffusion coefficient is
a(x,\alpha)=\alpha
.

5.3.
Model that satisfies the assumption [E3] and model that does not

First, as an example of a model that satisfies
[E3]
,
we consider the
d
-dimensional diffusion process with the diffusion coefficient

\displaystyle a(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\sigma(x)\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}),

where

\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\boldsymbol{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d})^{\mathsf{T}}
,
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{d}>0
.
The true value of the parameters are

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{c}_{1}

and

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}\boldsymbol{c}_{2}
,
where

\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}=(\alpha_{0,1},\ldots,\alpha_{0,3})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\boldsymbol{c}_{1}=(c_{1,1},\ldots,c_{1,d})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\boldsymbol{c}_{2}=(c_{2,1},\ldots,c_{2,d})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
Now we have

\displaystyle A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sigma(x)\mathrm{diag}(\alpha_{1}^{2},\ldots,\alpha_{d}^{2})\sigma(x)^{\mathsf{T}},

\displaystyle A^{-1}(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=[\sigma(x)^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}{\mathrm{diag}}(1/\alpha_{1}^{2},\ldots,1/\alpha_{d}^{2})\sigma(x)^{-1},

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{j}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\sigma(x)\mathrm{diag}(0,\ldots,2\alpha_{j},\ldots,0)\sigma(x)^{\mathsf{T}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha_{j}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{tr}\Bigl([\sigma(x)^{\mathsf{T}}]^{-1}\mathrm{diag}(0,\ldots,2/\alpha_{j},\ldots,0)\sigma(x)^{\mathsf{T}}\Bigr)=\frac{2}{\alpha_{j}},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Bigl[\mathrm{tr}\bigl(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha^{\ell}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0})\bigr)\Bigr]_{\ell}\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(x)(\boldsymbol{c}_{1}-\boldsymbol{c}_{2})=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{2(c_{1,j}-c_{2,j})}{\alpha_{0,j}}.

Therefore if

(5.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}}{\alpha_{j}}\neq 0,

then
[E3]
holds.
Especially, we have (
5.2
) if any of the following cases:

(1)

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}\geq 0
for all
1\leq j\leq d
,
and

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}>0
for some
1\leq j\leq d
.

(2)

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}\leq 0
for all
1\leq j\leq d
,
and

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}<0
for some
1\leq j\leq d
.

That is,
[E3]
holds when only
\alpha_{j}

(1\leq j\leq d)
changes.
From the above,
1-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process and hyperbolic diffusion model
are models which satisfy
[E3]

because the diffusion coefficient is
a(x,\alpha)=\alpha
.

Next, as an example of a model that does not satisfy
[E3]
,
we consider the 2-dimensional diffusion process with diffusion coefficient

\displaystyle a(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}\\
0&\alpha_{3}\end{array}\right),\quad(\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3}>0).

The true value of the parameters are

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\alpha_{0,1}\\
\alpha_{0,2}\\
\alpha_{0,3}\end{array}\right),\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}_{1}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1,1}\\
c_{1,2}\\
c_{1,3}\end{array}\right),\ \boldsymbol{\alpha}_{2}^{*}=\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{2,1}\\
c_{2,2}\\
c_{2,3}\end{array}\right).

Now we have

\displaystyle A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{1}&\alpha_{2}\\
0&\alpha_{3}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{1}&0\\
\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{3}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}&\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\\
\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{3}^{2}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle A^{-1}(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\alpha_{3}^{2}&-\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}\\
-\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}&\alpha_{1}^{2}+\alpha_{2}^{2}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{1}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2\alpha_{1}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right),\ \partial_{\alpha_{2}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}2\alpha_{2}&\alpha_{3}\\
\alpha_{3}&0\end{array}\right),\ \partial_{\alpha_{3}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\alpha_{2}\\
\alpha_{2}&2\alpha_{3}\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha_{1}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}}=\frac{2}{\alpha_{1}},

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha_{2}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}-\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}}=0,

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\left(A^{-1}\partial_{\alpha_{3}}A(x,\boldsymbol{\alpha})\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{-\alpha_{2}^{2}\alpha_{3}+2\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{3}+\alpha_{2}^{2}\alpha_{3}}{\alpha_{1}^{2}\alpha_{3}^{2}}=\frac{2}{\alpha_{3}},

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(\frac{2}{\alpha_{0,1}},0,\frac{2}{\alpha_{0,3}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{\boldsymbol{\alpha}_{0}}(x)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}c_{1,1}-c_{2,1}\\
c_{1,2}-c_{2,2}\\
c_{1,3}-c_{2,3}\end{array}\right)=\frac{2(c_{1,1}-c_{2,1})}{\alpha_{0,1}}+\frac{2(c_{1,3}-c_{2,3})}{\alpha_{0,3}}.

Therefore, (
5.3
) does not equal
0
in the following cases:

(1)

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}\geq 0
for all
j=1,3
,
and

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}>0
for some
j=1,3
.

(2)

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}\leq 0
for all
j=1,3
,
and

c_{1,j}-c_{2,j}<0
for some
j=1,3
.

However (
5.3
) equals
0
when

c_{1,1}-c_{2,1}=c_{1,3}-c_{2,3}=0
,
hence when only
\alpha_{2}
changes,
[E3]
does not hold.

5.4.
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process

We consider the 1-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\beta(X_{t}-\gamma)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=x_{0}.\quad(\alpha,\beta>0,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}).

In this subsection,
we refer to the consistency of tests
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}

in Case A.

First, let us consider the consistency of the test
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}

in Case A.
Thus, we consider the following stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\beta(X_{s}-\gamma)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{1}^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}-\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}\beta(X_{s}-\gamma)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

\alpha_{1}^{*}=\alpha_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}c_{1}

and

\alpha_{2}^{*}=\alpha_{0}+\vartheta_{\alpha}c_{2}
,
which hold
[E1]
.
Further, from Section
5.2
and
5.3
,

[E2]
and
[E3]
hold.
Therefore the test
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
is consistent by Proposition
1
.

Next, we investigate the consistency of the test
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}

in Case A.
Thus, we consider the following stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\beta_{1}^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma_{1}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}-\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}\beta_{2}^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma_{2}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{0}\\
\gamma_{0}\end{array}\right),\ {\boldsymbol{d}}_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{k,1}\\
d_{k,2}\end{array}\right),\ {\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{k}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{k}^{*}\\
\gamma_{k}^{*}\end{array}\right)={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}+\vartheta_{\beta}{\boldsymbol{d}}_{k},

which hold
[F1]
. Further,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}(-x+\gamma_{0},\beta_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})}(x)({\boldsymbol{d}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2})=\left(0,\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha^{*}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{1,1}-d_{2,1}\\
d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}\end{array}\right)=\frac{\beta_{0}}{\alpha^{*}}(d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}).

Therefore,
if
d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}\neq 0
, that is,
if
\gamma
changes and
\beta
does not change, then

[F3]
holds, and the test
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}
is consistent
by Proposition
2
.
However,
when
\beta
changes and
\gamma
does not change,

[F3]
does not hold.

5.5.
Hyperbolic diffusion model

We consider the hyperbolic diffusion model

(5.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\left(\beta-\frac{\gamma X_{t}}{\sqrt{1+X_{t}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=x_{0}.\quad(\alpha>0,\beta\in\mathbb{R},\gamma>|\beta|).

In this subsection,
we mention
the consistency of the tests

\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}
in Case A
and
the fact that this model is an example in Case B.

Let

b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})=\beta-\dfrac{\gamma x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}

and

a(x,\alpha)=\alpha
.
By the same discussion as Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process (Section
5.4
),
the test
\mathcal{T}_{n}^{\alpha}
is consistent in Case A.
Next, we investigate the consistency of the test
\mathcal{T}_{1,n}^{\beta}
in Case A.
Thus, we consider the following stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\beta_{1}^{*}-\frac{\gamma_{1}^{*}X_{s}}{\sqrt{1+X_{s}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}\left(\beta_{2}^{*}-\frac{\gamma_{2}^{*}X_{s}}{\sqrt{1+X_{s}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{0}\\
\gamma_{0}\end{array}\right),\ {\boldsymbol{d}}_{k}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{k,1}\\
d_{k,2}\end{array}\right),\ {\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{k}^{*}=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\beta_{k}^{*}\\
\gamma_{k}^{*}\end{array}\right)={\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{0}+\vartheta_{\beta}{\boldsymbol{d}}_{k},

which hold
[F1]
.
The invariant density of the solution in (
5.6
) is

\displaystyle\pi(x)=\frac{m(x)}{M},

where

\displaystyle m(x)=\exp\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta x-\gamma\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\right)\right),\ M=\int_{\mathbb{R}}m(x)\mathrm{d}x.

Now, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{\beta}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\pi(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\pi(x)\mathrm{d}x=1,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{\gamma}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\pi(x)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{M}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\exp\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta x-\gamma\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha^{2}}{2\gamma}\left(\frac{1}{M}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta-\frac{\gamma x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)\exp\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta x-\gamma\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x-\frac{2\beta}{\alpha^{2}}\right)

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta-\frac{\gamma x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)\exp\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta x-\gamma\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\right)\right)\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\left[\exp\left(\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\beta x-\gamma\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\right)\right)\right]_{x=-\infty}^{x=\infty}

\displaystyle=\left[\exp\left(-\frac{2\gamma}{\alpha^{2}}\sqrt{1+x^{2}}\left(1-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)\right)\right]_{x=-\infty}^{x=\infty}

\displaystyle=0.

Hence, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{\gamma}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\pi(x)\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{\beta}{\gamma}.

From the above, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}\left(1,-\frac{\beta_{0}}{\gamma_{0}}\right)\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})}(x)({\boldsymbol{d}}_{1}-{\boldsymbol{d}}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}\left(1,-\frac{\beta_{0}}{\gamma_{0}}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}d_{1,1}-d_{2,1}\\
d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}\end{array}\right)

(5.9)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{*}}\left((d_{1,1}-d_{2,1})-\frac{\beta_{0}}{\gamma_{0}}(d_{1,2}-d_{2,2})\right).

In the following cases,

[F3]
holds because equation (
5.9
) does not equal
0
:

(1)

\beta
changes and
\gamma
does not change,

(2)

\beta_{0}\neq 0
,

\gamma
changes and
\beta
does not change,

(3)

\beta_{0}>0
,

d_{1,1}-d_{2,1}<0
(resp.
>0
) and
d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}>0
(resp.
<0
),

(4)

\beta_{0}<0
,

d_{1,1}-d_{2,1}<0
(resp.
>0
) and
d_{1,2}-d_{2,2}<0
(resp.
>0
).

Finally, in Case B,
we confirm that the hyperbolic diffusion model satisfies the
assumptions

[B1-I]
,
[B2-I]
,
[B1-II]
and
[B2-II]
.
Note that it was mentioned in Remark
1

that
[B1-I]
and
[B2-I]
(a), (b) hold.
Thus, in the following, we verify that

[B2-I]
(c),
[B1-II]
and
[B2-II]
hold.
From the proof of Lemma 1 of Kessler (1997),
we can express
Q(x,\theta)=L_{\theta}^{2}f(y|x)|_{y=x}
,
where
f(y|x)=(y-x)^{2}
,

\displaystyle L_{\theta}f(y|x)

\displaystyle=b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})\partial_{y}f(y|x)+\frac{A(y,\alpha)}{2}\partial_{y}^{2}f(y|x),

\displaystyle L_{\theta}^{2}f(y|x)

\displaystyle=L_{\theta}[L_{\theta}f](y|x).

The specific calculation is as follows.

\displaystyle L_{\theta}f(y|x)

\displaystyle=2b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})(y-x)+\alpha^{2},

\displaystyle\partial_{y}[L_{\theta}f](y|x)

\displaystyle=2\bigl(\partial_{y}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})(y-x)+b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigr),

\displaystyle\partial_{y}^{2}[L_{\theta}f](y|x)

\displaystyle=2\bigl(\partial_{y}^{2}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})(y-x)+2\partial_{y}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigr),

\displaystyle L_{\theta}^{2}f(y|x)

\displaystyle=2b(y,\beta)\bigl(\partial_{y}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})(y-x)+b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigr)+\alpha^{2}\bigl(\partial_{y}^{2}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})(y-x)+2\partial_{y}b(y,\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigr),

and

Q(x,\theta)=2\bigl(b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})^{2}+\alpha^{2}\partial_{x}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\bigr)
.

b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})
and
\partial_{x}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})
are bounded,
so we have
\sup_{x,\theta}|Q(x,\theta)|<C
.
Thus,
[B2-I]
(c) holds.
Moreover, we have

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\left[\left(\beta_{1}-\frac{\gamma_{1}x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)-\left(\beta_{2}-\frac{\gamma_{2}x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)\right]^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)^{2},

where
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}=(\beta_{k},\gamma_{k})^{\mathsf{T}}
.
Therefore, since
-1<\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}<1
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
,
we have

\sup_{x}|\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*})|>0

in the following cases,
and
[B1-II]
holds.

(1)

\gamma_{1}^{*}=\gamma_{2}^{*}
,

(2)

\gamma_{1}^{*}\neq\gamma_{2}^{*}
and

\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*}<-(\gamma_{1}^{*}-\gamma_{2}^{*})
,

(3)

\gamma_{1}^{*}\neq\gamma_{2}^{*}
and

\beta_{1}^{*}-\beta_{2}^{*}>\gamma_{1}^{*}-\gamma_{2}^{*}
.

Furthermore, we see, from

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-2}{\alpha^{3}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\partial_{\beta_{1}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\alpha^{2}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right),

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})

\displaystyle=\frac{-2x}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right),

\partial_{\beta_{2}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})=-\partial_{\beta_{1}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})
,

\partial_{\gamma_{2}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})=-\partial_{\gamma_{1}}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\partial_{\beta}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\Bigl(b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})-b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\partial_{\gamma}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\Bigl(b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})-b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{-x}{\alpha^{2}\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\left((\beta_{1}-\beta_{2})-(\gamma_{1}-\gamma_{2})\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}\right)

and
the boundedness of
\dfrac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^{2}}}
,
that

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}}\left|\partial_{(\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})}\Gamma^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\right|<C,

\displaystyle\sup_{x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{k}}\left|\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\partial_{\boldsymbol{\beta}}b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta})\Bigl(b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1})-b(x,\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2})\Bigr)^{2}\right|<C

and

[B2-II]
holds.

6.
Simulations

6.1.
Case A

In this subsection, we consider the
1
-dimensional Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=-\beta(X_{t}-\gamma)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=x_{0},

where
\alpha,\beta>0,\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

In order to check the asymptotic behavior of the estimator
\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}

in Case A,
we consider the following stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\beta^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{1}^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}-\int_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}^{t}\beta^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\alpha}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

x_{0}=2
,

\beta^{*}=1
,
\gamma^{*}=2

and

\tau_{*}^{\alpha}=0.5
.
The number of iteration is 1000.
We set that the sample size of the data
\{X_{t_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is
n=10^{6}
and

h_{n}=n^{-2/3}=10^{-4}
.
Note that

T=nh_{n}=n^{1/3}=10^{2}
,

nh_{n}^{2}=n^{-1/3}=10^{-2}
,

\vartheta_{\alpha}=n^{-0.35}\approx 0.0079
,

n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}=n^{0.3}\approx 63.1
,

T\vartheta_{\alpha}=n^{-1/60}\approx 0.79
,

\alpha_{0}=0.1
,

\alpha_{1}^{*}=\alpha_{0}+n^{-0.35}

and

\alpha_{2}^{*}=\alpha_{0}=0.1
.
The existence of a change point in the intervals

[1/4T,T]
and
[0,3/4T]
was investigated using the method of
Tonaki et al. (2020).
In all 1000 iterations, the change point was detected.
Therefore, we estimated
\hat{\alpha}_{1}
and
\hat{\alpha}_{2}
with

\underline{\tau}^{\alpha}=1/4
and
\overline{\tau}^{\alpha}=3/4
, respectively.
The estimates of
\alpha_{1}^{*}
,
\alpha_{2}^{*}
and
\tau_{*}^{\alpha}

are reported in Table
1
.
Moreover, one has that
for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle e_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{\alpha}^{-1}(\alpha_{1}^{*}-\alpha_{2}^{*})=1,

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}e_{\alpha}^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Xi^{\alpha}(x,\alpha_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{\alpha_{0}}(x)e_{\alpha}=\frac{2}{\alpha_{0}^{2}},

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}(v)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}(v)+\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}|v|=-2\left(\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}(v)-\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}v|\right)\overset{d}{=}-2\left(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}v)-\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{J}_{\alpha}v|\right).

It follows from Theorem
1
that

\displaystyle n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{v\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\mathbb{F}(v).

For
v\in\mathbb{R}
, let
G(v)=\mathcal{W}(v)-\frac{1}{2}|v|

and
\hat{\eta}=\inf\left\{\eta\in\mathbb{R}\ |\ G(\eta)=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{R}}G(v)\right\}
.
For the probability density function of the distribution of
\hat{\eta}
,
see Lemma 1.6.3 of Csörgö and Horváth (1997).
From Figure
3
,
we can see that the distribution of the estimator almost
corresponds with the asymptotic distribution in Theorem
1

and
the estimators
have good performance.

,

,

For the simulations of the estimator
\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}

in Case A,
we treat the stochastic differential equation as follows.

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\beta^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma_{1}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}-\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}\beta^{*}(X_{s}-\gamma_{2}^{*})\mathrm{d}s+\alpha^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

x_{0}=5
,

\alpha^{*}=0.5
,

\beta^{*}=2.5

and

\tau_{*}^{\beta}=0.5
.
The number of iteration is 1000.
We set that the sample size of the data
\{X_{t_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is
n=10^{6}
and

h_{n}=n^{-4/7}\approx 3.73\times 10^{-4}
.
Note that

T=nh_{n}=n^{3/7}\approx 3.73\times 10^{2}
,

nh_{n}^{2}=n^{-1/7}\approx 0.14
,

\vartheta_{\beta}=n^{-1/8}\approx 0.18
,

T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}=n^{5/28}\approx 11.8
,

T\vartheta_{\beta}^{4}=n^{-1/14}\approx 0.37

\gamma_{1}^{*}=5+\vartheta_{\beta}

and

\gamma_{2}^{*}=5
.
In all iterations, the change point was detected in the intervals
[1/4T,T]

and
[0,3/4T]
.
Therefore, we estimated
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{1}
and
\hat{\boldsymbol{\beta}}_{2}

with
\underline{\tau}^{\beta}=1/4
and
\overline{\tau}^{\beta}=3/4
, respectively.
The estimates of
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*}
,
\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*}
and
\tau_{*}^{\beta}

are reported in Table
2
.
Noting that

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*}=(\beta^{*},\gamma_{1}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*}=(\beta^{*},\gamma_{2}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0}=(\beta^{*},\gamma_{2}^{*})^{\mathsf{T}}
,

\mu_{(\alpha^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})}\sim N\bigl(\gamma_{2}^{*},\frac{(\alpha^{*})^{2}}{2\beta^{*}}\bigr)

and

\displaystyle\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha,\boldsymbol{\beta})=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(x-\gamma)^{2}&-\beta(x-\gamma)\\
-\beta(x-\gamma)&\beta^{2}\end{array}\right),

we obtain that for
v\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle e_{\beta}

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_{\beta}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\beta}_{1}^{*}-\boldsymbol{\beta}_{2}^{*})=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\beta}

\displaystyle=e_{\beta}^{\mathsf{T}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Xi^{\beta}(x,\alpha^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})\mathrm{d}\mu_{(\alpha^{*},\boldsymbol{\beta}_{0})}(x)e_{\beta}=\frac{1}{(\alpha^{*})^{2}}(0,1)\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\frac{(\alpha^{*})^{2}}{2\beta^{*}}&0\\
0&(\beta^{*})^{2}\end{array}\right)\left(\begin{array}[]{c}0\\
1\end{array}\right)=\left(\frac{\beta^{*}}{\alpha^{*}}\right)^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{G}(v)

\displaystyle=-2\mathcal{J}_{\beta}^{1/2}\mathcal{W}(v)+\mathcal{J}_{\beta}|v|\overset{d}{=}-2\left(\mathcal{W}(\mathcal{J}_{\beta}v)-\frac{1}{2}|\mathcal{J}_{\beta}v|\right).

By Theorem
3
, we have that

\displaystyle T\vartheta_{\beta}^{2}(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\beta}-\tau_{*}^{\beta})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{v\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{argmin}}\,\mathbb{G}(v).

Since Figure
4
shows
that the distribution of the estimator is similar to the asymptotic distribution in Theorem
3
,
the estimator has good behavior.

6.2.
Case B

In this subsection, we consider the hyperbolic diffusion model

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\left(\beta-\frac{\gamma X_{t}}{\sqrt{1+X_{t}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}t+\alpha\mathrm{d}W_{t},\quad X_{0}=x_{0}.\quad(\alpha>0,\beta\in\mathbb{R},\gamma>|\beta|).

For simulations of the estimator
\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}
in Case B,
we study the following stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle X_{t}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle X_{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\left(\beta^{*}-\frac{\gamma^{*}X_{s}}{\sqrt{1+X_{s}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{1}^{*}W_{t},\quad t\in[0,\tau_{*}^{\beta}T),\\
\displaystyle X_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}+\int_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}^{t}\left(\beta^{*}-\frac{\gamma^{*}X_{s}}{\sqrt{1+X_{s}^{2}}}\right)\mathrm{d}s+\alpha_{2}^{*}(W_{t}-W_{\tau_{*}^{\beta}T}),\quad t\in[\tau_{*}^{\beta}T,T],\\
\end{cases}

where

x_{0}=2
,

\beta^{*}=0
,

,

,

,

.

\{X_{t_{i}}\}_{i=0}^{n}
is
n=10^{5}
or
10^{6}
and

h_{n}=n^{-2/3}
.

and

was investigated and the change point was detected.

\hat{\alpha}_{1}
and

, respectively.

.

2
, one has that

\displaystyle n(\hat{\tau}_{n}^{\alpha}-\tau_{*}^{\alpha})=O_{p}(1).

shows

in this example.

7.
Proofs

Let
\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}=\sigma\left[\{W_{s}\}_{s\leq t_{i}^{n}}\right]
, and

C
,
C^{\prime}>0
denote universal constants.
If

f
is a function on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Theta
,

f_{i-1}(\theta)

denotes
the value
f(X_{t_{i-1}},\theta)
.
If
\{u_{n}\}
is a positive sequence,
R
denotes a function
on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\times\Theta

for which there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in\Theta}\|R(x,u_{n},\theta)\|\leq u_{n}C(1+\|x\|)^{C}.

Let
R_{i-1}(u_{n},\theta)=R(X_{t_{i-1}},u_{n},\theta)
.
We set

\displaystyle A\otimes x^{\otimes k}=\sum_{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}=1}^{d_{1}}A^{\ell_{1},\ldots,\ell_{k}}x^{\ell_{1}}\cdots x^{\ell_{k}},\quad\text{ for }A\in\underbrace{\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}}_{k},\ x\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{1}}.

Let
\Upsilon_{n}(\tau:\theta_{1},\theta_{2})
be a contrast function,
and let
\hat{\theta}_{1}
,
\hat{\theta}_{2}
be estimators of
\theta_{1}
,
\theta_{2}
,
respectively,
and let

\displaystyle\hat{\tau}_{n}=\mathrm{argmin}_{\tau\in[0,1]}\Upsilon_{n}(\tau:\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2})

be the estimator of
\tau^{*}
, and let

\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)=\Upsilon_{n}(\tau^{*}+v/r_{n}:\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2})-\Upsilon_{n}(\tau^{*}:\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2})
.
If there exist a positive sequence
\{r_{n}\}

with
r_{n}\longrightarrow\infty

and
a random field
\mathbb{H}(v)

that satisfy the following conditions, then

\displaystyle r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{argmin}_{v\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}(v)
.

(a)

r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})=O_{p}(1)
,

(b)

For all
L>0
,

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle w}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{H}(v)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{D}[-L,L].

Proof.

Set

\displaystyle v^{\dagger}=\mathrm{argmin}_{v\in\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{H}(v)
.
For all
x\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle P(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x,\ r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\in[-L,L],\ \inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)>\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\right)

(7.1)

\displaystyle\quad+P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\right).

If

r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\in[-L,L]

and

\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)>\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)
,
then

\displaystyle\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\Upsilon_{n}\left(\tau^{*}+\dfrac{v}{r_{n}}:\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2}\right)>\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\Upsilon_{n}\left(\tau^{*}+\dfrac{v}{r_{n}}:\hat{\theta}_{1},\hat{\theta}_{2}\right),

hence, from
\tau^{*}+\dfrac{x}{r_{n}}<\hat{\tau}_{n}\leq\tau^{*}+\dfrac{L}{r_{n}}
,

x<r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq L

and

\displaystyle P\left(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x,\ r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\in[-L,L],\ \inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)>\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x,\ x<r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq L\Bigr)

\displaystyle=0.

From (b), as
n\to\infty
, (
7.1
) is evaluated as follows.

\displaystyle\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\right)

(7.2)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v)\right).

Now we have

\displaystyle P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v)\right)

\displaystyle\leq P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v),\ v^{\dagger}\in[-L,L],\ v^{\dagger}>x\right)

\displaystyle\quad+P(v^{\dagger}\notin[-L,L])+P(v^{\dagger}\leq x).

Because if

\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v)

and
v^{\dagger}\in[-L,L]
,
then
-L\leq v^{\dagger}\leq x
,
we have

\displaystyle P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v),\ v^{\dagger}\in[-L,L],\ v^{\dagger}>x\right)\leq P(-L\leq v^{\dagger}\leq x,\ v^{\dagger}>x)=0.

Therefore from

\displaystyle P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\leq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v)\right)\leq P(v^{\dagger}\notin[-L,L])+P(v^{\dagger}\leq x)

and (a), as
L\to\infty
, (
7.2
) is evaluated as follows.

\displaystyle\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varlimsup_{L\to\infty}\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+\varlimsup_{L\to\infty}P(v^{\dagger}\notin[-L,L])+P(v^{\dagger}\leq x)

(7.3)

\displaystyle=P(v^{\dagger}\leq x).

In the same way, we have

\displaystyle\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})>x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\leq\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\geq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\hat{\mathbb{H}}_{n}(v)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+P\left(\inf_{v\in[-L,x]}\mathbb{H}(v)\geq\inf_{v\in[x,L]}\mathbb{H}(v)\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\notin[-L,L]\Bigr)+P(v^{\dagger}>x)+P(v^{\dagger}\notin[-L,L]).

As
L\to\infty
,

\displaystyle\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})>x\Bigr)\leq P(v^{\dagger}>x),

i.e.,

\displaystyle\varliminf_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\varliminf_{n\to\infty}\Bigl[1-P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})>x\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle=1-\varlimsup_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})>x\Bigr)

\displaystyle\geq 1-P(v^{\dagger}>x)

(7.4)

\displaystyle=P(v^{\dagger}\leq x).

From (
7.3
) and (
7.4
), we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}P\Bigl(r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\leq x\Bigr)=P(v^{\dagger}\leq x)

and
r_{n}(\hat{\tau}_{n}-\tau^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}v^{\dagger}
.
∎

In Case A of Situation I, we set

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{n}(v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{n}\left(\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+\frac{v}{n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}:\alpha_{1}^{*},\alpha_{2}^{*}\right)-\Phi_{n}\left(\tau_{*}^{\alpha}:\alpha_{1}^{*},\alpha_{2}^{*}\right),

\displaystyle\hat{\mathbb{F}}_{n}(v)

\displaystyle=\Phi_{n}\left(\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+\frac{v}{n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}:\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2}\right)-\Phi_{n}\left(\tau_{*}^{\alpha}:\hat{\alpha}_{1},\hat{\alpha}_{2}\right),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\alpha}(v)

\displaystyle=\hat{\mathbb{F}}_{n}(v)-\mathbb{F}_{n}(v).

Suppose that
[C1]
-
[C5]
,
[C6-I]
,

[A1-I]
and
[A2-I]
hold.
Then, for all
L>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in[-L,L]}|\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\alpha}(v)|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.

We assume that
v>0
. Then, we can express

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{n}^{\alpha}(v)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\Bigl(F_{i}(\hat{\alpha}_{1})-F_{i}(\hat{\alpha}_{2})\Bigr)-\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\Bigl(F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})-F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\left[\left(F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})+\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*})+\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\otimes(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad-\left(F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*})+\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*})(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*})+\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\otimes(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\qquad-\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\Bigl(F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})-F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*})\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\Bigl(\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*})(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*})+\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*})(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*})\Bigr)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{1}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\otimes(\hat{\alpha}_{1}-\alpha_{1}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\right.

(7.5)

\displaystyle\left.\qquad\qquad-\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{2}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\otimes(\hat{\alpha}_{2}-\alpha_{2}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\right).

Now we see

\displaystyle\sup_{v\in[0,L]}\left|\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\otimes(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*})^{\otimes 2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+L/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\left|\int_{0}^{1}\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*}+u(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}))\mathrm{d}u\right||\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+L/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\sup_{\alpha\in\Theta_{A}}\left|\partial_{\alpha}^{2}F_{i}(\alpha)\right|\left(\sqrt{n}|\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}|\right)^{2}

(7.6)

\displaystyle=O_{p}\left(\frac{1}{n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}\right)=o_{p}(1)

and

\displaystyle\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*})

(7.7)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\Bigl(\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})-\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}[\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}]\Bigr)(\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*})+\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}[\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}](\hat{\alpha}_{k}-\alpha_{k}^{*}).

By Theorem 2.11 of Hall and Heyde (1980), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}\left[\frac{1}{n}\sup_{v\in[0,L]}\left|\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+v/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\left(\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})-\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}[\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}]\right)\right|^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{n}\sum_{i=[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}]+1}^{[n\tau_{*}^{\alpha}+L/\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}]}\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}\left[\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}\left[\left|\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})-\mathbb{E}_{\alpha_{2}^{*}}[\partial_{\alpha}F_{i}(\alpha_{k}^{*})|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}]\right|^{2}\middle|\mathscr{G}_{i-1}^{n}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C^{\prime}}{n\vartheta_{\alpha}^{2}}\longrightarrow 0

and

(7.8)
